The fact that these beliefs have been persistent for over a year and have not been accompanied by hallucinations or other psychotic symptoms supports this. The presence of additional suspicious beliefs about others potentially covering for her husband could indicate a broader paranoid component, but the primary focus on a single, fixed delusion (the husband's affair) keeps both F22 (persistent delusional disorder) and F23 (acute and transient psychotic disorders, considering the gradual development) as candidates. However, the absence of reported hallucinations, disorganized behavior, or significant mood episodes, and the fact that the delusion has been consistent over time without clear evidence of acute onset, slightly leans the diagnosis towards F22. The patient's ability to maintain daily routines and lack of reported anxiety or depressive episodes that could have triggered these beliefs also supports the consideration of a delusional disorder. I know in my gut what's happening, but my husband and the kids are just covering for him.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other thoughts or beliefs that you're completely sure about, despite others telling you they're not true, or is this situation with your husband the only one that's been bothering you?\n\nPatient: It really all comes back to my husband, though I have started questioning the motives of people in my walking group and at church lately. I can't help but feel like some of them are helping him cover his tracks or keeping secrets for him. But yes, his affair is the main thing I am absolutely certain of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any periods where these beliefs about your husband and the others seemed less convincing or even completely untrue to you, or have they remained consistently convincing since they started?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never had any doubts or moments where it seemed less real to me. My certainty has been absolute from the very beginning, and it has remained completely consistent for over a year now. No matter what my family or anyone else says to convince me otherwise, I know what I know.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any hallucinations, such as hearing voices or seeing things that others cannot, or have your beliefs and suspicions been the only unusual experiences you've had?\n\nPatient: Absolutely not, I've never heard voices or seen things that aren't there, and my mind isn't playing tricks on me. I don't have any \"unusual experiences\" or mental issues like that. My only concern is the very real, concrete situation with my husband's behavior.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've noticed any changes in your daily life or behavior as a result of these beliefs, such as avoiding certain places or people, or has your daily routine remained relatively unchanged despite these convictions?\n\nPatient: My basic routine is mostly fine, and I still do my housework and home-organization jobs without any issues. However, I have started avoiding certain choir gatherings and walking group outings so I don't have to see my husband or that woman. It has also really strained things with my kids, who don't visit as much anymore because they are tired of hearing me talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any episodes where you felt extremely overwhelmed, anxious, or depressed, which might have triggered or worsened these beliefs about your husband, or have these beliefs developed gradually over time without any specific triggering events?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any episodes of anxiety or depression that triggered this. It actually started very gradually about eighteen months ago, right around the time my adult children became more independent and I noticed him doing things like changing his phone password. Over the next year, those small observations slowly turned into the absolute certainty I have today.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any periods where you felt like your thoughts or actions were being controlled or influenced by an outside force, or have you experienced any other unusual or unexplainable events that might be related to your beliefs about your husband?\n\nPatient: No, I've never felt controlled by outside forces or experienced anything bizarre like that. My beliefs are based entirely on the concrete things I've actually observed, like him changing his phone password, lingering after choir, and whispering with that younger woman. Those real actions are the sole basis for everything I know.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your daily life and relationships have changed since you became certain about your husband's affair, and are there any times when you feel like you're able to put these thoughts aside or focus on other things?"
  }

My relationships have definitely suffered; I've skipped some walking groups to avoid them, and my kids visit less because they're tired of the arguments. As long as the topic isn't brought up, I feel completely like my usual self and function just fine.

I know in my gut what's happening, but my husband and the kids are just covering for him.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other thoughts or beliefs that you're completely sure about, despite others telling you they're not true, or is this situation with your husband the only one that's been bothering you?\n\nPatient: It really all comes back to my husband, though I have started questioning the motives of people in my walking group and at church lately. I can't help but feel like some of them are helping him cover his tracks or keeping secrets for him. But yes, his affair is the main thing I am absolutely certain of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any periods where these beliefs about your husband and the others seemed less convincing or even completely untrue to you, or have they remained consistently convincing since they started?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never had any doubts or moments where it seemed less real to me. My certainty has been absolute from the very beginning, and it has remained completely consistent for over a year now. No matter what my family or anyone else says to convince me otherwise, I know what I know.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any hallucinations, such as hearing voices or seeing things that others cannot, or have your beliefs and suspicions been the only unusual experiences you've had?\n\nPatient: Absolutely not, I've never heard voices or seen things that aren't there, and my mind isn't playing tricks on me. I don't have any \"unusual experiences\" or mental issues like that. My only concern is the very real, concrete situation with my husband's behavior.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've noticed any changes in your daily life or behavior as a result of these beliefs, such as avoiding certain places or people, or has your daily routine remained relatively unchanged despite these convictions?\n\nPatient: My basic routine is mostly fine, and I still do my housework and home-organization jobs without any issues. However, I have started avoiding certain choir gatherings and walking group outings so I don't have to see my husband or that woman. It has also really strained things with my kids, who don't visit as much anymore because they are tired of hearing me talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any episodes where you felt extremely overwhelmed, anxious, or depressed, which might have triggered or worsened these beliefs about your husband, or have these beliefs developed gradually over time without any specific triggering events?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any episodes of anxiety or depression that triggered this. It actually started very gradually about eighteen months ago, right around the time my adult children became more independent and I noticed him doing things like changing his phone password. Over the next year, those small observations slowly turned into the absolute certainty I have today.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any periods where you felt like your thoughts or actions were being controlled or influenced by an outside force, or have you experienced any other unusual or unexplainable events that might be related to your beliefs about your husband?\n\nPatient: No, I've never felt controlled by outside forces or experienced anything bizarre like that. My beliefs are based entirely on the concrete things I've actually observed, like him changing his phone password, lingering after choir, and whispering with that younger woman. Those real actions are the sole basis for everything I know.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your daily life and relationships have changed since you became certain about your husband's affair, and are there any times when you feel like you're able to put these thoughts aside or focus on other things?\n\nPatient: My relationships have definitely suffered; I've skipped some walking groups to avoid them, and my kids visit less because they're tired of the arguments. However, I can easily put these thoughts aside to focus on my housework, my home-organization jobs, or choir when they aren't around. As long as the topic isn't brought up, I feel completely like my usual self and function just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other fixed beliefs that are not related to your husband's supposed affair, such as beliefs about your health, your surroundings, or other personal relationships, or has your certainty about your husband's infidelity been the only persistent belief?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any unusual beliefs about my health or anything unrelated like that. My absolute certainty is strictly about my husband sneaking around and having an affair. The only other thing is that I do feel like some of our family and church friends are acting suspiciously and trying to cover for him."
  }

The fact that they have no hallucinations, their daily life and behavior are relatively unchanged except for avoidance of certain social situations, and they can focus on other aspects of their life when the topic isn't brought up, points more towards a delusional disorder rather than a psychotic disorder. The gradual development of the belief over time without any triggering events of anxiety or depression also supports this. The previous candidate of 'F23' seems less likely as the patient does not exhibit disorganized thinking, behavior, or affect, which are characteristic of schizophrenia spectrum disorders. Therefore, 'F22' remains as a candidate, and 'F23' is less likely based on the information provided.", "is_final": false}

But yes, his affair is the main thing I am absolutely certain of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any periods where these beliefs about your husband and the others seemed less convincing or even completely untrue to you, or have they remained consistently convincing since they started?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never had any doubts or moments where it seemed less real to me. My certainty has been absolute from the very beginning, and it has remained completely consistent for over a year now. No matter what my family or anyone else says to convince me otherwise, I know what I know.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any hallucinations, such as hearing voices or seeing things that others cannot, or have your beliefs and suspicions been the only unusual experiences you've had?\n\nPatient: Absolutely not, I've never heard voices or seen things that aren't there, and my mind isn't playing tricks on me. I don't have any \"unusual experiences\" or mental issues like that. My only concern is the very real, concrete situation with my husband's behavior.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've noticed any changes in your daily life or behavior as a result of these beliefs, such as avoiding certain places or people, or has your daily routine remained relatively unchanged despite these convictions?\n\nPatient: My basic routine is mostly fine, and I still do my housework and home-organization jobs without any issues. However, I have started avoiding certain choir gatherings and walking group outings so I don't have to see my husband or that woman. It has also really strained things with my kids, who don't visit as much anymore because they are tired of hearing me talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any episodes where you felt extremely overwhelmed, anxious, or depressed, which might have triggered or worsened these beliefs about your husband, or have these beliefs developed gradually over time without any specific triggering events?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any episodes of anxiety or depression that triggered this. It actually started very gradually about eighteen months ago, right around the time my adult children became more independent and I noticed him doing things like changing his phone password. Over the next year, those small observations slowly turned into the absolute certainty I have today.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any periods where you felt like your thoughts or actions were being controlled or influenced by an outside force, or have you experienced any other unusual or unexplainable events that might be related to your beliefs about your husband?\n\nPatient: No, I've never felt controlled by outside forces or experienced anything bizarre like that. My beliefs are based entirely on the concrete things I've actually observed, like him changing his phone password, lingering after choir, and whispering with that younger woman. Those real actions are the sole basis for everything I know.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your daily life and relationships have changed since you became certain about your husband's affair, and are there any times when you feel like you're able to put these thoughts aside or focus on other things?\n\nPatient: My relationships have definitely suffered; I've skipped some walking groups to avoid them, and my kids visit less because they're tired of the arguments. However, I can easily put these thoughts aside to focus on my housework, my home-organization jobs, or choir when they aren't around. As long as the topic isn't brought up, I feel completely like my usual self and function just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other fixed beliefs that are not related to your husband's supposed affair, such as beliefs about your health, your surroundings, or other personal relationships, or has your certainty about your husband's infidelity been the only persistent belief?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any unusual beliefs about my health or anything unrelated like that. My absolute certainty is strictly about my husband sneaking around and having an affair. The only other thing is that I do feel like some of our family and church friends are acting suspiciously and trying to cover for him.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility, even if just for a moment, that your beliefs about your husband's affair might not be entirely accurate, or do you remain completely convinced of their truth?\n\nPatient: No, I have never doubted it for even a second, and I remain one hundred percent convinced of the truth. I know everyone keeps telling me I'm wrong or overreacting, but I just cannot shake this certainty in my gut. Absolutely nothing anyone says will change my mind about what my husband is hiding.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've ever felt like these beliefs about your husband are part of a larger system or plot, or if they're just isolated to his actions and the people directly involved?\n\nPatient: No, it's not some grand, massive plot or conspiracy at all. It is strictly about my husband's actions, that younger woman from church, and the people close to us who are helping him cover his tracks. This is a very specific, real situation happening right in front of me, not some crazy system."
  }
]

My certainty has been absolute from the very beginning, and it has remained completely consistent for over a year now. No matter what my family or anyone else says to convince me otherwise, I know what I know.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any hallucinations, such as hearing voices or seeing things that others cannot, or have your beliefs and suspicions been the only unusual experiences you've had?\n\nPatient: Absolutely not, I've never heard voices or seen things that aren't there, and my mind isn't playing tricks on me. I don't have any \"unusual experiences\" or mental issues like that. My only concern is the very real, concrete situation with my husband's behavior.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've noticed any changes in your daily life or behavior as a result of these beliefs, such as avoiding certain places or people, or has your daily routine remained relatively unchanged despite these convictions?\n\nPatient: My basic routine is mostly fine, and I still do my housework and home-organization jobs without any issues. However, I have started avoiding certain choir gatherings and walking group outings so I don't have to see my husband or that woman. It has also really strained things with my kids, who don't visit as much anymore because they are tired of hearing me talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any episodes where you felt extremely overwhelmed, anxious, or depressed, which might have triggered or worsened these beliefs about your husband, or have these beliefs developed gradually over time without any specific triggering events?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any episodes of anxiety or depression that triggered this. It actually started very gradually about eighteen months ago, right around the time my adult children became more independent and I noticed him doing things like changing his phone password. Over the next year, those small observations slowly turned into the absolute certainty I have today.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any periods where you felt like your thoughts or actions were being controlled or influenced by an outside force, or have you experienced any other unusual or unexplainable events that might be related to your beliefs about your husband?\n\nPatient: No, I've never felt controlled by outside forces or experienced anything bizarre like that. My beliefs are based entirely on the concrete things I've actually observed, like him changing his phone password, lingering after choir, and whispering with that younger woman. Those real actions are the sole basis for everything I know.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your daily life and relationships have changed since you became certain about your husband's affair, and are there any times when you feel like you're able to put these thoughts aside or focus on other things?\n\nPatient: My relationships have definitely suffered; I've skipped some walking groups to avoid them, and my kids visit less because they're tired of the arguments. However, I can easily put these thoughts aside to focus on my housework, my home-organization jobs, or choir when they aren't around. As long as the topic isn't brought up, I feel completely like my usual self and function just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other fixed beliefs that are not related to your husband's supposed affair, such as beliefs about your health, your surroundings, or other personal relationships, or has your certainty about your husband's infidelity been the only persistent belief?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any unusual beliefs about my health or anything unrelated like that. My absolute certainty is strictly about my husband sneaking around and having an affair. The only other thing is that I do feel like some of our family and church friends are acting suspiciously and trying to cover for him.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered the possibility, even if just for a moment, that your beliefs about your husband's affair might not be entirely accurate, or do you remain completely convinced of their truth?\n\nPatient: No, I have never doubted it for even a second, and I remain one hundred percent convinced of the truth. I know everyone keeps telling me I'm wrong or overreacting, but I just cannot shake this certainty in my gut. Absolutely nothing anyone says will change my mind about what my husband is hiding.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've ever felt like these beliefs about your husband are part of a larger system or plot, or if they're just isolated to his actions and the people directly involved?\n\nPatient: No, it's not some grand, massive plot or conspiracy at all. It is strictly about my husband's actions, that younger woman from church, and the people close to us who are helping him cover his tracks. This is a very specific, real situation happening right in front of me, not some crazy system.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your husband's supposed affair is somehow related to you being specially chosen or targeted, or is it just a simple case of infidelity in your view?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel specially chosen or targeted in any way; it's just a straightforward case of my husband betraying me and sneaking around. What frustrates me most is the betrayal itself and the fact that my own kids and friends are helping him cover his tracks instead of believing me. It's not some grand conspiracy against me, just a very painful, real situation in my marriage."

Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nMy basic routine is mostly fine, and I still do my housework and home-organization jobs without any issues. However, I have started avoiding certain choir gatherings and walking group outings so I don't have to see my husband or that woman. It has also really strained things with my kids, who don't visit as much anymore because they are tired of hearing me talk about it.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
